Navigation Example tasks Track errors with Sentry Example tasks Track errors with Sentry Copy page Copy page This example demonstrates how to track errors with Sentry using Trigger.dev. Copy page Copy page ​ Overview Automatically send errors and source maps to your Sentry project from your Trigger.dev tasks. Sending source maps to Sentry allows for more detailed stack traces when errors occur, as Sentry can map the minified code back to the original source code. ​ Prerequisites A Sentry account and project A Trigger.dev account and project ​ Setup This setup involves two files: trigger.config.ts - Configures the build to upload source maps to Sentry during deployment trigger/init.ts - Initializes Sentry and registers the error tracking hook at runtime You will need to set the SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN and SENTRY_DSN environment variables. You can find the SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN in your Sentry dashboard, in settings -&gt; developer settings -&gt; auth tokens and the SENTRY_DSN in your Sentry dashboard, in settings -&gt; projects -&gt; your project -&gt; client keys (DSN). Add these to your .env file, and in your Trigger.dev dashboard , under environment variables in your project’s sidebar. ​ Build configuration Add this build configuration to your trigger.config.ts file. This uses the Sentry esbuild plugin to upload source maps every time you deploy your project. trigger.config.ts import { defineConfig } from &quot;@trigger.dev/sdk&quot; ; import { esbuildPlugin } from &quot;@trigger.dev/build/extensions&quot; ; import { sentryEsbuildPlugin } from &quot;@sentry/esbuild-plugin&quot; ; export default defineConfig ({ project : &quot;&lt;project ref&gt;&quot; , // Your other config settings... build : { extensions : [ esbuildPlugin ( sentryEsbuildPlugin ({ org : &quot;&lt;your-sentry-org&gt;&quot; , project : &quot;&lt;your-sentry-project&gt;&quot; , // Find this auth token in settings -&gt; developer settings -&gt; auth tokens authToken : process . env . SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN , }) , { placement : &quot;last&quot; , target : &quot;deploy&quot; } ) , ] , } , }); Build extensions allow you to hook into the build system and customize the build process or the resulting bundle and container image (in the case of deploying). You can use pre-built extensions or create your own. ​ Bun runtime If you are using the Bun runtime, esbuild’s bundling of @sentry/node ’s CJS entry can cause a runtime error in the local dev environment. Add the following extension to mark @sentry/node as external in dev only: trigger.config.ts import { defineConfig } from &quot;@trigger.dev/sdk&quot; ; import { esbuildPlugin } from &quot;@trigger.dev/build/extensions&quot; ; import { sentryEsbuildPlugin } from &quot;@sentry/esbuild-plugin&quot; ; export default defineConfig ({ project : &quot;&lt;project ref&gt;&quot; , runtime : &quot;bun&quot; , build : { extensions : [ { name : &quot;sentry-external-dev&quot; , externalsForTarget : (target) =&gt; (target === &quot;dev&quot; ? [ &quot;@sentry/node&quot; ] : []) , } , esbuildPlugin ( sentryEsbuildPlugin ({ org : &quot;&lt;your-sentry-org&gt;&quot; , project : &quot;&lt;your-sentry-project&gt;&quot; , authToken : process . env . SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN , }) , { placement : &quot;last&quot; , target : &quot;deploy&quot; } ) , ] , } , }); This lets Bun resolve @sentry/node directly from node_modules during dev, while still bundling it normally for deployment. ​ Runtime initialization Create a trigger/init.ts file to initialize Sentry and register the global onFailure hook. This file is automatically loaded when your tasks execute. trigger/init.ts import { tasks } from &quot;@trigger.dev/sdk&quot; ; import * as Sentry from &quot;@sentry/node&quot; ; // Initialize Sentry Sentry .init ({ defaultIntegrations : false , // The Data Source Name (DSN) is a unique identifier for your Sentry project. dsn : process . env . SENTRY_DSN , // Update this to match the environment you want to track errors for environment : process . env . NODE_ENV === &quot;production&quot; ? &quot;production&quot; : &quot;development&quot; , }); // Register a global onFailure hook to capture errors tasks .onFailure (({ payload , error , ctx }) =&gt; { Sentry .captureException (error , { extra : { payload , ctx , } , }); }); Learn more about global lifecycle hooks and the init.ts file . ​ Testing that errors are being sent to Sentry To test that errors are being sent to Sentry, you need to create a task that will fail. This task takes no payload, and will throw an error. trigger/sentry-error-test.ts import { task } from &quot;@trigger.dev/sdk&quot; ; export const sentryErrorTest = task ({ id : &quot;sentry-error-test&quot; , retry : { // Only retry once maxAttempts : 1 , } , run : async () =&gt; { const error = new Error ( &quot;This is a custom error that Sentry will capture&quot; ); error .cause = { additionalContext : &quot;This is additional context&quot; }; throw error; } , }); After creating the task, deploy your project. npm pnpm yarn npx trigger.dev@latest deploy pnpm dlx trigger.dev@latest deploy yarn dlx trigger.dev@latest deploy Once deployed, open the sentry-error-test task in your Trigger.dev dashboard (make sure you’re in your prod environment) and press the “Test” button to open the test page for it. Run a test task with an empty payload by clicking the Run test button. Your run should then fail, and if everything is set up correctly, you will see an error in the Sentry project dashboard shortly after. Was this page helpful?
